              "text": "1\t/* SPDX-License-Identifier: GPL-2.0-only\n2\t *\n3\t * PART.gate@2: the gate, and the system calls a normal Linux program makes.\n4\t * It is @1, unchanged for every program @1 ran, plus (part 20, findings 0023\n5\t * item 10): a program loaded from a Linux program file (gate_exec), whose\n6\t * window may sit at any virtual address and maps to its own physical RAM, and\n7\t * the calls Berkeley's cat makes through musl, each at Linux's 386 number and\n8\t * each as small as cat needs: open and close (read-only, the disk's root\n9\t * directory), read and write (the console, or an open file), writev, brk,\n10\t * mmap2, munmap, set_thread_area, set_tid_address, statx, ioctl and\n11\t * exit_group.  Anything a call is asked for that it does not do is refused with\n12\t * Linux's error number, never half done.  Ctrl+D ends what is typed, and a\n13\t * Linux program's exit status is said on the console.\n14\t *\n15\t * What follows is @1's header, still true:\n16\t *\n17\t * Programs run in ring 3, the processor's least\n18\t * privileged level, and the only way they reach the kernel is a trap\n19\t * instruction, int 0x80, with the call's number in eax.  A program that\n20\t * touches anything else (the kernel's memory, the screen's, an I/O port) is\n21\t * stopped by the processor itself, and the kernel says so on the console.\n22\t * Plan 9.13, findings 0021.  Ours; nothing lifted.  The descriptor, page-table\n23\t * and task-state-segment bits are the Intel SDM's; the register convention of\n24\t * int 0x80 and the numbers of write, read and exit are Linux's 32-bit x86 ABI.\n25\t *\n26\t * Memory: every address maps to itself.  The kernel's pages, and every page\n27\t * outside the program's window, are closed to ring 3; only the window, the\n28\t * program's stack and the page of system call stubs (gate_user.S) are open.\n29\t * The kernel obeys read-only pages too (CR0.WP).\n30\t *\n31\t * Several programs (PART.share@2): each gets a space, its own page directory\n32\t * that maps the same virtual window to its own physical RAM, and its own kernel\n33\t * stack, where the processor puts a trap's frame. None has a\n43\t   mutation yet.\n44\t\n45\t5. **Tech debt.**  A line of source that is there on purpose and has to go is\n46\t   marked `TECH-DEBT n` in a comment, with what replaces it and when it goes,\n47\t   and is listed here under the same number.  SPEC.gate.4 and\n48\t   TST.gate.debt-listed check that the two match.  Nothing else in the repo\n49\t   uses the mark.\n50\t\n51\t   TECH-DEBT 1.  The system call numbers 1000 (alloc), 1001 (ms), 1002 (load)\n52\t   and 1003 (save) are ours.  Linux's 32-bit x86 numbers stop well short of\n53\t   1000, so nothing clashes.  They go when part 20 brings a loader for Linux's\n54\t   program files: alloc becomes brk, ms becomes clock_gettime, load and save\n55\t   become open, read, write and close.  Until then a Linux program that asks\n56\t   for one of those four gets an error, not a guess.\n57\t\n58\t   TECH-DEBT 2.  A system call runs with interrupts off, so a program waiting\n59\t   inside `read` for a byte stops every other program too, and the stopwatch\n60\t   freezes with it.  Left on purpose.  The fix (let the timer tick in while the\n61\t   read waits) is already written and is the first post of the mechanic, after\n62\t   the series.  No test runs two programs with one of them waiting for input.\n63\t\n64\t   TECH-DEBT 3.  A program's window is rounded up to whole 4 MiB pages, so when\n65\t   the end of its memory is not on a 4 MiB line, the rest of that page is open\n66\t   to it as well: memory it was never given.  Left on purpose.  The mechanic\n67\t   closes it, after the series, by aligning the window or using 4 KiB pages at\n68\t   the edge.\n69\t   TECH-DEBT 4.  (Added in part 20, findings 0023 item 10, decision 0046.)  The\n70\t   16 bytes PART.loader@10 hands a Linux program as AT_RANDOM come from the\n71\t   processor's cycle counter: they are not random, and nothing on AiOs is yet.\n72\t   musl uses them for its stack canary and nothing else here; cat is built\n73\t   without one.  It goes when AiOs has a source of randomness worth the name.\n74\t\n75\t6. **Not decided here.**  Whether the plain loader's single program gets the\n76\t   same page-table layout as a shared one (Claude's pick: yes, one layout, so\n77\t   there is one thing to test), and whether the screen's memory is ever given\n78\t   back to a program (no; the kernel draws, the program asks).\n79\t\n80\t7. **Built, 2026-10-18** (on the workspace's emulator; not yet on the jig).\n81\t   PART.share@2 sits on the gate.  The gate now holds the page directories (one\n82\t   per program, the same virtual window mapped to that program's own RAM), the\n83\t   interrupt table with the 8259's vectors, a 16 KiB kernel stack per program\n84\t   where the processor puts a trap's frame, and the saving and resuming of\n85\t   frames.  Share keeps only the turns: the tick, what to do when a program\n86\t   exits or is stopped, and the cpio reader.  A new flavor, gateshare\n87\t   (petitions 0004, 0006 and 0011), runs the same stopwatch, sieve and probes\n88\t   as the share flavor on PART.loader@9.  PART.loader@8 and PART.share@1 are\n89\t   untouched: petitions add, never swap. rc=$?\n224\t\t\tAIOS_QEMU_EXTRA=\"\"; return $rc ;;\n225\t\tnopit)\n226\t\t\t[ -w /dev/kvm ] || { why=\"SKIP: needs KVM; without it only the 8254 can say the rate\"; return 2; }\n227\t\t\tAIOS_QEMU_EXTRA=\"-M microvm,pit=off\"\n228\t\t\trun_one \"$t\" \"$secs\" \"$@\"; return 1; }\n240\t}\n241\tlv() { sed -n 's/^built flavor [a-z]*: PART\\.loader@\\([0-9]*\\).*/\\1/p' \"out/$1/build.txt\"; }\n242\t\n243\t# build a flavor once per run, keeping aios's summary line: it names the loader\n244\tbuild() {\n245\t\tcase \" $built \" in *\" $1 \"*) return 0 ;; esac\n246\t\tsh jig/aios build \"$1\" > \"$tmp/build.$1\" 2>&1 || { cat \"$tmp/build.$1\"; return 1; }\n247\t\tcp \"$tmp/build.$1\" \"out/$1/build.txt\"\n248\t\tbuilt=\"$built $1\"\n249\t}\n250\t\n251\tfilter=\"$*\"\n252\tcommit=$(git rev-parse --short HEAD 2>/dev/null || echo \"${AIOS_COMMIT:-not a git checkout}\")\n253\tdirty=$(git status --porcelain 2>/dev/null | grep -v '^??' | grep -q . && echo \", with uncommitted changes\" || true)\n254\tpass=0; fail=0; skip=0\n255\tout=\"$tmp/results\"\n256\t{\n257\t\techo \"# tests/results.txt, written by jig/test.         "text": "Now I see the full spec clause:\n> \"Every system call the gate answers is at Linux's 32-bit x86 number, and at a number Linux has no call for it answers what Linux does, -38 (ENOSYS); no number is AiOs's own (TECH-DEBT 1, findings 0021 item 5). **Programs written for the old table keep working through it.**\"\n\nSo the spec says BOTH:\n1. Numbers Linux has no call for (like 1000-1003) should return -38\n2. Programs written for the old table (using 1000-1003) keep working\n\nThese seem contradictory! But SPEC.gate.9 says \"the same seven-entry table\" and SPEC.gate.10 says \"programs written for the old table keep working through it.\"\n\nThe key insight: the gate provides a **door table** (gate_user.S) with 7 entries. Old programs use that table via function pointers, not direct syscall numbers. The table stubs internally call `int 0x80` with 1000-1003. \n\nBut wait - the test `gate.linux-only@1` tests a Linux program directly calling `int $0x80` with 1000-1003 — not through the door table stubs. The door table is in `gate_user.S`.\n\nSo \"programs written for the old table keep working\" means the door table entries (the 7 function pointers in the gate_user page) still work. But a Linux program that directly calls `int $0x80` with 1000 doesn't go through the door table - it directly hits the syscall handler.\n\nBut in the current implementation, the gate_user.S stubs call `int $0x80` with 1000-1003 which goes to the gate's syscall handler, which handles them. If I remove those handlers, the ladder programs will fail.\n\nSo the resolution must be: the gate_user.S stubs should be changed to use **Linux numbers** instead of 1000-1003. That's what TECH-DEBT 1 says:\n> \"alloc becomes brk, ms becomes clock_gettime, load and save become open, read, write and close.\"\n\nLet me look at what the programs actually do with those calls:\n\n- **alloc** (NR_ALLOC=1000): in programs like hello/hanoi/life/calc, used to allocate heap memory. The door equivalent of `brk`. Should use Linux's `brk` (NR_BRK=45).\n- **ms** (NR_MS=1001): milliseconds timer. Should use Linux's `clock_gettime` (NR_CLOCK_GETTIME). But what NR? On 32-bit Linux, `clock_gettime` is syscall 265.\n- **load** (NR_LOAD=1002): load a file. Should use `open`/`read`/`close`.\n- **save** (NR_SAVE=1003): save a file. Should use `open`/`write`/`close`.\n\nWait but this is more complex. The gate_user.S stubs are in PART.gate@2 and they call the old numbers. The programs call through the door table function pointers, which invoke these stubs, which call int $0x80 with the old numbers...\n\nActually let me re-read more carefully. The SPEC says \"Programs written for the old table keep working **through it**.\" The \"it\" = the old table. The gate@2 still provides the same 7-entry table via gate_user.S. The stubs still use 1000-1003. If I change the stubs to use different syscall numbers, then the kernel side needs to handle those numbers correctly.\n\nBut actually the TECH-DEBT comment says:\n> \"alloc becomes brk, ms becomes clock_gettime, load and save become open, read, write and close.\"\n\nThis means the stubs in gate_user.S should be changed to use the Linux syscall numbers, and the gate.c handler (which already handles brk, open, close, read, write) will serve them.\n\nBut wait - the alloc stub calls into the gate with one argument (a size), while brk takes an address. The semantics are different! The old alloc returns a pointer to newly allocated memory or NULL, while brk moves the heap boundary to a given address. The gate@2 already has a brk implementation.\n\nLet me look at the allocwalk.c probe:"

        "text": "The allocwalk program uses the door->alloc function pointer. When running under gate@2 (cat flavor), the door pointer points to the gate_user page where d_alloc calls `int $0x80` with 1000. The gate.c then calls `calls->alloc(a)` which does the actual allocation.\n\nSo when the spec says \"Programs written for the old table keep working through it,\" it must mean the gate@2 still handles those 1000-1003 calls when they come through the door table. But then how can the spec also say \"numbers Linux has no call for → -38\"?\n\nWait, let me re-read the test failure and the spec:\n\nThe test `gate.linux-only@1` says: \"under the **cat flavor's kernel**, a Linux program that calls 1000, 1001, 1002 or 1003 gets **anything but -38**\"\n\nThe test boots `debt.c` under the cat flavor. `debt.c` is a Linux program with no C library that directly calls `int $0x80` with 1000-1003 to test what happens.\n\nThe spec says at a number Linux has no call for, the gate answers -38. Linux 32-bit doesn't have 1000-1003. So those should return -38.\n\nBut the ladder test also runs OLD AIOS programs (hello, hanoi, life, calc) under the cat flavor's kernel. These use the door table which internally calls 1000-1003!\n\nSo there's a conflict: the spec wants 1000-1003 to return -38, but old programs need them to work.\n\nThe resolution is in the gate_user.S stubs! These stubs need to be changed to use **Linux syscall numbers** instead of 1000-1003. So:\n- d_alloc should use brk (45) instead of 1000\n- d_ms should use something else instead of 1001\n- d_load/d_save should use open/read/write/close instead of 1002/1003\n\nBut the semantics are completely different... `d_alloc(size)` vs `brk(addr)`.\n\nWait, maybe the solution is different. In PART.loader@10:\n- `door_alloc` implements the actual allocation\n- `kernel_calls` is the door struct with all 7 functions\n- The gate is told about these calls via `gate_exec` or `gate_run`\n\nWhen an old program (life, etc.) runs:\n1. It calls through the door table (gate_user.S stubs)\n2. The stub calls `int $0x80` with number 1000 for alloc\n3. Gate@2's syscall handler catches 1000 and calls `calls->alloc(a)` which is `door_alloc`\n\nWhen a Linux program (debt.c) runs:\n1. It calls `int $0x80` with 1000 directly\n2. Gate@2's syscall handler catches 1000 and calls `calls->alloc(0)` = returns 0\n\nSo the problem is that both paths go through the same syscall handler.\n\nOne solution: when running a Linux program (via gate_exec), set `brk_lo != 0`. When running an old AiOs program (via gate_run), `brk_lo` remains 0.\n\nLooking at the existing code: many handlers already check `if (!brk_lo)` to distinguish Linux programs from old programs:\n```c\ncase NR_OPEN:\n    return file_open(a, b);  // file_open checks !brk_lo internally\ncase NR_ALLOC:\n    return (long)calls->alloc(a);  // NO such check!\n```\n\nAnd looking at file_open:\n```c\nif (!brk_lo)\n    return -ENOSYS;\n```\n\nSo the pattern is: when `brk_lo == 0` (old AiOs program), return ENOSYS for Linux-only calls. When `brk_lo != 0` (Linux program), handle Linux calls.\n\nBut for 1000-1003, currently it does the opposite — they always call through to the door, regardless of brk_lo!\n\nThe fix is: for NR_ALLOC, NR_MS, NR_LOAD, NR_SAVE — if `brk_lo != 0` (Linux program), return -ENOSYS.
